===== PAGE 3 =====
NATIONAL ARCHIVES AND RECORDS ADMINISTRATION 
MICROFILM PUBLICATIONS 
INTRODUCTION 
On this single roll of microfilm publication, M1657, are reproduced records of the 
Naval Air Advanced Training Command in Jacksonville, Florida. These records are 
located at the National Archives-Southwest Region in Fort Worth, Texas. They are 
part of the Records of Naval Districts and Shore Establishments, Record Group 181. 
Background 
The Air Operational Training Command was established on April 30, 1942, with 
headquarters at Jacksonville Naval Air Station to process naval aviators through an 
advanced course to prepare them for assignment to fleet units and to conduct 
advanced and spec ialized flight and ground training. An Air Intermediate Training 
Command was established on October l , 1942, with headquarters at the Naval Air 
Station at Pensacola and operations at the Corpus Christi Naval Air Station. Both of 
these Commands became part of the Naval Air Training Comma nd when it was 
established at the Pensacola NAS on December 20, 1943. 
On November 1, 1945, the Air Intermediate Training Command was redesignated 
the Naval Air Basic Training Command, and the Air Operational Training 
Command became the Naval Air Advanced Training Command. Its headquarters 
were moved from Jacksonville NAS to Corpus Christi, Texas, on November 24, 
1948. 
At 2: 10 p.m. on December 5, 1945, five Navy Avenger Torpedo Bombers 
disappeared while on a training mission over the Bermuda Triangle. The Bermuda 
Triangle is an area that covers 440,000 square miles between the Bermuda Islands , 
the coast of Southern Florida, and San Juan, Puerto Rico. The airplanes departed that 
fateful afternoon from the U.S. Naval Air Station in Fort Lauderdale, Florida, on 
what has become known as Flight 19. The airplanes were on a routine navigation 
exercise over the Atlantic Ocean. At about 4 p.m. a radio message was received 
from Flight 19. The message stated that they were lost and uncertain of the direction 
to the Florida coast. There was also some indication that the compasses on the 
airplanes were malfunctioning. Due to radio traffic, communication with Flight 19 
was difficult. After several attempts to lead the ill-fated flight to safety, radio contact 
was lost and the flight was never beard from again. 
A massive search was launched over the next 5 days for the lost squadron. Over 200 
airplanes a day searched for the missing crew. Despite this enormous search effort, 
no trace of the airplanes or the crew was ever found. In fact, one of the PBM patrol 
planes involved in the search for Flight 19 also vanished on December 5, 1945. On 
December IO, 1945, the search was called off with a total of27 men and six 
airplanes still missing. A Board oflnvestigation convened at the U.S. Naval Air 
Station on December l 0, 1945, and subsequently released their Findings of Fact. 
Although the Findings of Fact point to factors that may have contributed to the loss 
of the airplanes, the disappearance of Flight 19 remains one of the greatest unsolved 
mysteries of the Bennuda Triangle. 

===== PAGE 4 =====
NATIONAL ARCHIVES AND RECORDS ADMINISTRATION 
MICROFILM PUBLICATIONS 
Records Description 
The records that follow are related to this incident and include weather observations, 
the history of the aircraft and engine logs, a rough crash log, radio station logs, pre­
flight forms, communication logs, incident reports, air/sea rescue plans, maps of the 
search area, and the Board oflnvestigation's Findings of Fact.
